etc_m_family_abs

U6_S4_ETC_03

U6_S4_ETC_03 · Exit Ticket C · Mastery · etc_m_family_equation_abs · etc-3 · Equation · constructed/open response

Identify the function family represented by $f(x)=|x-4|+2$ and name one feature of the equation that supports your choice.

Answer / solution

Absolute value. The absolute-value bars show the parent family; the expression represents a translated absolute-value function.

Alignment / evidence

Exact target: I can apply family features to sort representations into linear, quadratic, exponential, absolute value, and other familiar forms.

Representation: Equation

Expected evidence/job: Portfolio evidence slice for U6-MG01: Identify common function families using evidence from graphs, equations, tables, contexts, and key features.

Source basis: Unit 6 Assessment Plan Mastery/portfolio evidence route + Algebra 1 Framework source section 4.1

Mastery Goal: U6-MG01 — Identify common function families using evidence from graphs, equations, tables, contexts, and key features.

etc_m_family_exponential

U6_S4_ETC_02

U6_S4_ETC_02 · Exit Ticket C · Mastery · etc_m_family_table_exponential · etc-2 · Table · constructed/open response

Identify the function family supported by the table and cite the pattern that supports your choice.

$x$$y$
$0$$3$
$1$$6$
$2$$12$
$3$$24$
Answer / solution

Exponential. The outputs have a constant multiplicative ratio of $2$.

Alignment / evidence

Exact target: I can apply family features to sort representations into linear, quadratic, exponential, absolute value, and other familiar forms.

Representation: Table

Expected evidence/job: Portfolio evidence slice for U6-MG01: Identify common function families using evidence from graphs, equations, tables, contexts, and key features.

Source basis: Unit 6 Assessment Plan Mastery/portfolio evidence route + Algebra 1 Framework source section 4.1

Answer / solution

Alignment / evidence

U6_S3_EX_02

U6_S3_EX_02 · Notes Example · Intro · quadratic_formula_rational · example-2 · Equation · constructed/open response

Use the quadratic formula to solve $x^2-5x+6=0$.

Answer / solution

$x=\frac{5\pm\sqrt{25-24}}{2}=\frac{5\pm1}{2}$, so $x=2,3$.

Alignment / evidence

Exact target: I can apply the quadratic formula to solve equations with rational, irrational, or no real solutions.

eta_p_error_zero_product

U6_S1_ETA_04

U6_S1_ETA_04 · Exit Ticket A · Intro · eta_p_error_zero_product · eta-4 · Equation + Prose · constructed/open response

A student solves $(x-11)(x+6)=0$ and reports $x=-11,6$. Diagnose the error and give the correct solutions.

Answer / solution

The student copied the factor constants instead of solving each factor equation. Correct: $x=11,-6$.

Alignment / evidence

Exact target: I can apply the zero product property to solve quadratic equations in factored form.

Representation: Equation + Prose

Expected evidence/job: Short independent Intro evidence for the mapped current-section I-can.

Source basis: Unit 6 Assessment Plan + Algebra 1 Framework v6.34

wtc_factor_zero_bridge

U6_S1_WTC_01

U6_S1_WTC_01 · WTC · Intro · wtc_factor_zero_bridge · shared-graph · Graph + Equation · constructed/open response

A parabola is shown below. The same relationship can be written as $y=(x-2)(x+4)$.

  1. Estimate the two $x$-intercepts from the graph.
  2. What values of $x$ make $(x-2)(x+4)=0$?
  3. Explain why the answers to the first two parts should match.
Answer / solution

The intercepts and solutions are $x=2$ and $x=-4$. At an $x$-intercept, $y=0$, so the product $(x-2)(x+4)$ must equal zero.

Alignment / evidence

Exact target: I can apply the zero product property to solve quadratic equations in factored form.

Representation: Graph + Equation

Expected evidence/job: Preview the bridge from factors/zeros to solving a quadratic equation.

Source basis: Unit 6 Assessment Plan + Algebra 1 Framework v6.34

sum_transform_equation_point

U6_SUM_V2_Q02

U6_SUM_V2_Q02 · Secure Summative V2 · Mastery · sum_transform_point_table_infer · v2-5 · Table + Equation + Key points · constructed/open response

The table pairs points on the parent $y=x^2$ with their images on a transformed graph. Determine the coordinate transformation rule, write the transformed equation, and explain how one row verifies your rule.

Parent pointImage point
$(-2,4)$$(-3,0)$
$(0,0)$$(-1,-2)$
$(2,4)$$(1,0)$
Answer / solution

The rule is $(x,y)\to(x-1,\frac12y-2)$. This is a vertical compression by $\frac12$, left $1$, and down $2$. A matching equation is $y=\frac12(x+1)^2-2$. Any listed row correctly verifies the rule.

Alignment / evidence

Exact target: I can apply transformation rules to key points and features across function families.

Representation: Table + Equation + Key points

Expected evidence/job: Independent secure evidence for U6-MG02: Represent translations, stretches, compressions, and reflections of parent functions across graphs, equations, tables, and key points.

Source basis: Unit 6 Assessment Plan Summative mastery evidence + Algebra 1 Framework Unit 4 supporting learning

Mastery Goal: U6-MG02 — Represent translations, stretches, compressions, and reflections of parent functions across graphs, equations, tables, and key points.

U6_SUM_V3_Q04

U6_SUM_V3_Q04 · Secure Summative V3 · Mastery · sum_transform_equation_point · v3-1 · Equation + Key point · constructed/open response

Describe the transformations from $y=x^2$ to $y=-2(x-1)^2$. Then map the parent point $(1,1)$ to its new location.

Answer / solution

Shift right $1$, reflect across the $x$-axis, and vertically stretch by factor $2$. The parent point $(1,1)$ maps to $(2,-2)$.

Alignment / evidence

Exact target: I can apply transformation rules to key points and features across function families.

Representation: Equation + Key point

wtc_solution_count_graph

U6_S3_WTC_01

U6_S3_WTC_01 · WTC · Intro · wtc_solution_count_graph · three-graphs · Graph + Equation · constructed/open response

Three quadratic graphs, labeled $f$, $g$, and $h$, are shown on the same axes.

  1. How many real solutions would $f(x)=0$, $g(x)=0$, and $h(x)=0$ have?
  2. What graph feature tells you the number of real solutions?
  3. Predict what information in an equation might help determine this without graphing.
Answer / solution

$f(x)=0$ has 2 real solutions, $g(x)=0$ has 1, and $h(x)=0$ has 0. The number of $x$-intercepts gives the number of real solutions. A useful equation-based quantity would need to distinguish these three cases; the discriminant will do that later in the section.

Alignment / evidence

Exact target: I can assess how the discriminant predicts the number and type of real solutions.

Representation: Graph + Equation

Expected evidence/job: Preview the connection between real solution count and graph intercepts before formal discriminant work.

Source basis: Unit 6 Assessment Plan + Algebra 1 Framework v6.34

model_reasonableness_domain

U6_S5_EX_06

U6_S5_EX_06 · Notes Example · Intro · model_reasonableness_domain · example-6 · Context + Equation · constructed/open response

A revenue model is $R(p)=-20(p-15)^2+4500$ for prices $5\le p\le25$. Interpret the vertex and explain why values outside the domain should not be used.

Answer / solution

The vertex says the model predicts maximum revenue $4500$ at price $p=15$. The stated domain is part of the model; extrapolating outside it is unsupported by the situation.

Alignment / evidence

Exact target: I can assess whether a quadratic model and its solutions are reasonable for the situation.

Representation: Context + Equation

Expected evidence/job: Teacher-facing worked instructional anchor for the mapped I-can.

A height model is valid only for $0\le t\le6$ and has a mathematical zero at $t=-1$. Should that zero be reported as a physical event? Explain.

Answer / solution

No. $t=-1$ lies outside the physical domain, so it is not a meaningful event for the modeled motion.

sum_transform_equation_point

U6_SUM_V1_Q02

U6_SUM_V1_Q02 · Secure Summative V1 · Mastery · sum_transform_equation_point · v1-1 · Equation + Key point · constructed/open response

Describe the transformations from $y=x^2$ to $y=-2(x-1)^2+1$. Then map the parent point $(1,1)$ to its new location.

Answer / solution

Shift right $1$, reflect across the $x$-axis, vertically stretch by factor $2$, and shift up $1$. The parent point $(1,1)$ maps to $(2,-1)$.

Alignment / evidence

Exact target: I can assess whether an equation, graph, or table represents the same transformed function.

Representation: Equation + Key point

Expected evidence/job: Independent secure evidence for U6-MG02: Represent translations, stretches, compressions, and reflections of parent functions across graphs, equations, tables, and key points.

Source basis: Unit 6 Assessment Plan Summative mastery evidence + Algebra 1 Framework Unit 4 supporting learning

U6_SUM_V3_Q07

U6_SUM_V3_Q07 · Secure Summative V3 · Mastery · sum_transform_point_table_infer · v3-5 · Table + Equation + Key points · constructed/open response

The table pairs points on the parent $y=x^2$ with their images on a transformed graph. Determine the coordinate transformation rule, write the transformed equation, and explain how one row verifies your rule.

Parent pointImage point
$(-2,4)$$(1,1)$
$(0,0)$$(3,-1)$
$(2,4)$$(5,1)$
Answer / solution

The rule is $(x,y)\to(x+3,\frac12y-1)$. This is a vertical compression by $\frac12$, right $3$, and down $1$. A matching equation is $y=\frac12(x-3)^2-1$. Any listed row correctly verifies the rule.

Alignment / evidence

Exact target: I can apply transformation rules to key points and features across function families.

Representation: Table + Equation + Key points

Expected evidence/job: Independent secure evidence for U6-MG02: Represent translations, stretches, compressions, and reflections of parent functions across graphs, equations, tables, and key points.

Source basis: Unit 6 Assessment Plan Summative mastery evidence + Algebra 1 Framework Unit 4 supporting learning

Mastery Goal: U6-MG02 — Represent translations, stretches, compressions, and reflections of parent functions across graphs, equations, tables, and key points.

I can build perfect-square trinomials by adding the needed constant term.

complete_square_area_model

U6_S2_EX_05

U6_S2_EX_05 · Notes Example · Intro · complete_square_area_model · example-5 · Diagram + Equation · constructed/open response

Use the area model to explain why $x^2+8x+16$ is a perfect square.

Answer / solution

The side lengths are $x+4$ by $x+4$, so the total area is $(x+4)^2$.

Alignment / evidence

Exact target: I can build perfect-square trinomials by adding the needed constant term.

Representation: Diagram + Equation

Expected evidence/job: Teacher-facing worked instructional anchor for the mapped I-can.

Source basis: Unit 6 Assessment Plan + Algebra 1 Framework v6.34

U6_S2_YTI_05

U6_S2_YTI_05 · Notes YTI · Intro · complete_square_area_model · yti-5 · Diagram + Equation · constructed/open response

Use the area model to write the total expression as a perfect square.

Answer / solution

$x^2+4x+4=(x+2)^2$.

Alignment / evidence

Exact target: I can build perfect-square trinomials by adding the needed constant term.

Representation: Diagram + Equation

Expected evidence/job: Immediate application of the just-modeled idea with changed values or representation.

Source basis: Unit 6 Assessment Plan + Algebra 1 Framework v6.34

complete_square_constant

U6_S2_EX_01

U6_S2_EX_01 · Notes Example · Intro · complete_square_constant · example-1 · Equation · constructed/open response

Find the constant that makes $x^2+10x+\square$ a perfect-square trinomial, then factor it.

Answer / solution

Half of 10 is 5, and $5^2=25$. Thus $x^2+10x+25=(x+5)^2$.

Alignment / evidence

Exact target: I can build perfect-square trinomials by adding the needed constant term.

wtc_complete_square_area

U6_S2_WTC_01

U6_S2_WTC_01 · WTC · Intro · wtc_complete_square_area · area-model · Diagram + Equation · constructed/open response

The diagram shows a square built from one $x\times x$ region, two $x\times3$ strips, and one small square.

  1. Write an expression for the total area.
  2. Why is the total also $(x+3)^2$?
  3. If the corner $9$ were missing, what constant would complete the square?
Answer / solution

The total area is $x^2+6x+9$, which is $(x+3)^2$. The missing corner must have area $9$.

Alignment / evidence

Exact target: I can build perfect-square trinomials by adding the needed constant term.

Representation: Diagram + Equation

Expected evidence/job: Preview the geometric meaning of the constant that completes a square.

Source basis: Unit 6 Assessment Plan + Algebra 1 Framework v6.34

sum_alt_equivalent_quad_forms

U6_SUM_V5_Q05

U6_SUM_V5_Q05 · Secure Summative V5 · Mastery · sum_alt_equivalent_quad_forms · v5-5 · Equations + Graph features · constructed/open response

Compare $f(x)=(x+2)(x-6)$ and $g(x)=(x-2)^2-16$. Show that they represent the same quadratic, then state the zeros, axis of symmetry, and direction of opening. Explain which form reveals each feature most directly.

Answer / solution

Both expand to $x^2-4x-12$, so they are equivalent. The zeros are $x=-2,6$; the axis of symmetry is $x=2$; the graph opens upward. Factored form reveals the zeros most directly, while vertex form reveals the axis/vertex structure; the positive leading coefficient shows the opening direction.

Alignment / evidence

Exact target: I can construct connections between polynomial structure, quadratic graphs, and key features.

Representation: Equations + Graph features

Expected evidence/job: Independent alternate-form secure evidence for U6-MG04: Connect polynomial and quadratic expression structure to graph features such as vertex, intercepts, symmetry, and direction of opening.

Source basis: Unit 6 Assessment Plan Summative mastery evidence + Algebra 1 Framework Unit 4 supporting learning

Mastery Goal: U6-MG04 — Connect polynomial and quadratic expression structure to graph features such as vertex, intercepts, symmetry, and direction of opening.

U6_SUM_V6_Q07

U6_SUM_V6_Q07 · Secure Summative V6 · Mastery · sum_alt_equivalent_quad_forms · v6-7 · Equations + Graph features · constructed/open response

For $f(x)=x^2-6x+5$, rewrite the expression in vertex form and factored form. Use the two forms to state the vertex and $x$-intercepts, then explain which form reveals each feature most directly.

Answer / solution

Vertex form: $f(x)=(x-3)^2-4$, so the vertex is $(3,-4)$. Factored form: $f(x)=(x-1)(x-5)$, so the $x$-intercepts are $(1,0)$ and $(5,0)$. Vertex form reveals the vertex directly; factored form reveals the zeros/intercepts directly.

Alignment / evidence

Exact target: I can construct connections between polynomial structure, quadratic graphs, and key features.

Representation: Equations + Graph features

Expected evidence/job: Independent alternate-form secure evidence for U6-MG04: Connect polynomial and quadratic expression structure to graph features such as vertex, intercepts, symmetry, and direction of opening.

Source basis: Unit 6 Assessment Plan Summative mastery evidence + Algebra 1 Framework Unit 4 supporting learning

Mastery Goal: U6-MG04 — Connect polynomial and quadratic expression structure to graph features such as vertex, intercepts, symmetry, and direction of opening.

sum_quad_structure_features

U6_SUM_V2_Q04

U6_SUM_V2_Q04 · Secure Summative V2 · Mastery · sum_quad_vertex_from_standard · v2-7 · Equivalent equations + Graph features · constructed/open response

Rewrite $f(x)=x^2+4x-5$ in vertex form. Then use that form to state the vertex, axis of symmetry, and the minimum value of $f$.

Answer / solution

$f(x)=(x+2)^2-9$. The vertex is $(-2,-9)$, the axis of symmetry is $x=-2$, and the minimum value is $-9$.

Alignment / evidence

U6_SUM_V3_Q08

U6_SUM_V3_Q08 · Secure Summative V3 · Mastery · sum_quad_structure_features · v3-3 · Equation + Graph features · constructed/open response

For $f(x)=(x+1)(x-4)$, state the x-intercepts, direction of opening, and axis of symmetry. Explain which features came directly from structure and which required one extra step.

Answer / solution

Intercepts $(-1,0),(4,0)$; opens upward; axis $x=1.5$. The intercepts and opening come directly from factored form; the axis is the midpoint of the zeros.

Alignment / evidence

Exact target: I can construct connections between polynomial structure, quadratic graphs, and key features.

Representation: Equation + Graph features

Expected evidence/job: Independent secure evidence for U6-MG04: Connect polynomial and quadratic expression structure to graph features such as vertex, intercepts, symmetry, and direction of opening.

sum_quad_table_structure

U6_SUM_V6_Q04

U6_SUM_V6_Q04 · Secure Summative V6 · Mastery · sum_quad_table_structure · v6-4 · Table + Equation + Graph features · constructed/open response

Use the table to identify the vertex, axis of symmetry, zeros, and direction of opening. Then write a quadratic equation consistent with the data.

$x$$y$
$-2$$0$
$-1$$-3$
$0$$-4$
$1$$-3$
$2$$0$
Answer / solution

The vertex is $(0,-4)$, the axis of symmetry is $x=0$, the zeros are $x=-2,2$, and the graph opens upward. One matching equation is $y=x^2-4$.

Alignment / evidence

Exact target: I can construct connections between polynomial structure, quadratic graphs, and key features.

Representation: Table + Equation + Graph features

Expected evidence/job: Independent alternate-form secure evidence for U6-MG04: Connect polynomial and quadratic expression structure to graph features such as vertex, intercepts, symmetry, and direction of opening.

The table comes from a quadratic function.

$x$$y$
$-2$$0$
$-1$$-4$
$0$$-6$
$2$$-4$
$3$$0$

Use the table to identify the zeros and write a possible factored equation with leading coefficient 1.

Answer / solution

Zeros: $-2$ and $3$. A possible equation is $y=(x+2)(x-3)$.

construct_model_vertex_point

U6_S5_EX_02

U6_S5_EX_02 · Notes Example · Intro · construct_model_vertex_point · example-2 · Equation + Key features · constructed/open response

A parabola has vertex $(3,20)$ and passes through $(1,12)$. Write a vertex-form model.

Answer / solution

Use $y=a(x-3)^2+20$. Then $12=4a+20$, so $a=-2$. Model: $y=-2(x-3)^2+20$.

Alignment / evidence

Exact target: I can construct quadratic models from real-world constraints, data, or key features.

Representation: Equation + Key features

Expected evidence/job: Teacher-facing worked instructional anchor for the mapped I-can.

Source basis: Unit 6 Assessment Plan + Algebra 1 Framework v6.34

A parabola has vertex $(-2,-5)$ and passes through $(0,3)$. Write a vertex-form model.

Answer / solution

$3=4a-5$, so $a=2$. Model: $y=2(x+2)^2-5$.

construct_model_zeros_point

U6_S5_EX_01

U6_S5_EX_01 · Notes Example · Intro · construct_model_zeros_point · example-1 · Equation + Key features · constructed/open response

A quadratic has zeros $2$ and $8$ and passes through $(0,16)$. Construct a model.

Answer / solution

Start $y=a(x-2)(x-8)$. Using $(0,16)$: $16=16a$, so $a=1$. Thus $y=(x-2)(x-8)$.

Alignment / evidence

Exact target: I can construct quadratic models from real-world constraints, data, or key features.

Representation: Equation + Key features

Expected evidence/job: Teacher-facing worked instructional anchor for the mapped I-can.

Source basis: Unit 6 Assessment Plan + Algebra 1 Framework v6.34

U6_S5_YTI_01

U6_S5_YTI_01 · Notes YTI · Intro · construct_model_zeros_point · yti-1 · Equation + Key features · constructed/open response

A quadratic has zeros $-1$ and $5$ and passes through $(0,-5)$. Construct a model.

Answer / solution

$y=a(x+1)(x-5)$ and $-5=-5a$, so $a=1$. Thus $y=(x+1)(x-5)$.

Alignment / evidence

Exact target: I can construct quadratic models from real-world constraints, data, or key features.

critique_method_efficiency

U6_S4_EX_04

U6_S4_EX_04 · Notes Example · Intro · critique_method_efficiency · example-4 · Equation + Prose · constructed/open response

A student uses the quadratic formula on $x^2-9=0$. The work can be correct. Explain why another method is more efficient.

Answer / solution

Difference of squares gives $(x-3)(x+3)=0$ immediately, so $x=\pm3$ with less arithmetic and fewer opportunities for substitution errors.

Alignment / evidence

Exact target: I can critique a selected solution method for efficiency, accuracy, and fit to the equation.

Representation: Equation + Prose

Expected evidence/job: Teacher-facing worked instructional anchor for the mapped I-can.

Source basis: Unit 6 Assessment Plan + Algebra 1 Framework v6.34

U6_S4_YTI_04

U6_S4_YTI_04 · Notes YTI · Intro · critique_method_efficiency · yti-4 · Equation + Prose · constructed/open response

A student completes the square to solve $x^2-5x+6=0$. Explain why factoring may be more efficient.

Answer / solution

$x^2-5x+6=(x-2)(x-3)$, so factoring reaches $x=2,3$ directly.

Alignment / evidence

Exact target: I can critique a selected solution method for efficiency, accuracy, and fit to the equation.

Compare solving $x^2-100=0$ by factoring and by the quadratic formula. Which is more efficient and why?

cyu_p_critique_default_method

U6_S4_CYU_06

U6_S4_CYU_06 · CYU · Intro · cyu_p_critique_default_method · cyu-6 · Equation + Prose · constructed/open response

A student chooses the quadratic formula for $2x^2+7x-4=0$. Critique the choice and identify a more efficient method if you see one.

Answer / solution

The quadratic formula works, but factoring is shorter because $2x^2+7x-4=(2x-1)(x+4)$. The solutions are $x=\frac12$ and $x=-4$.

Alignment / evidence

exact_vs_approx_method

U6_S4_EX_06

U6_S4_EX_06 · Notes Example · Intro · exact_vs_approx_method · example-6 · Context + Equation · constructed/open response

A measurement model leads to $2x^2-3x-4=0$. The final report needs a decimal to the nearest tenth. Choose a method and explain how exact and approximate answers can both be useful.

Answer / solution

Use the quadratic formula for the exact solutions $x=\frac{3\pm\sqrt{41}}{4}$, then approximate at the end: about $2.4$ and $-0.9$. Exact form preserves full precision; decimals support measurement interpretation.

Alignment / evidence

Exact target: I can critique a selected solution method for efficiency, accuracy, and fit to the equation.

Representation: Context + Equation

Expected evidence/job: Teacher-facing worked instructional anchor for the mapped I-can.

Source basis: Unit 6 Assessment Plan + Algebra 1 Framework v6.34

I can critique whether a polynomial expression supports a claimed vertex, intercept, axis of symmetry, or direction of opening.

etc_m_critique_opening

U6_S1_ETC_02

U6_S1_ETC_02 · Exit Ticket C · Mastery · etc_m_critique_opening · etc-2 · Equation + Prose · constructed/open response

A student claims $g(x)=-2(x-3)^2+1$ opens upward. Critique the claim.

Answer / solution

The leading multiplier is negative, so the parabola opens downward. The claim is false.

Alignment / evidence

Exact target: I can critique whether a polynomial expression supports a claimed vertex, intercept, axis of symmetry, or direction of opening.

Representation: Equation + Prose

U6_S1_P3_22

U6_S1_P3_22 · Practice Set 3 · Mastery · m_critique_opening · p3-22 · Equation + Prose · constructed/open response

A student claims $g(x)=-(x+3)^2+5$ opens upward. Critique the claim.

Answer / solution

The leading multiplier is negative, so the parabola opens downward. The claim is false.

sum_quad_claim_critique

U6_SUM_V5_Q02

U6_SUM_V5_Q02 · Secure Summative V5 · Mastery · sum_quad_claim_critique · v5-2 · Equation + Prose · constructed/open response

A student claims $y=(x-3)^2-8$ has an x-intercept at $(3,0)$ because 3 appears in the equation. Critique the claim and identify the feature that 3 actually reveals.

Answer / solution

The claim is false. The 3 gives the vertex x-coordinate, so the vertex is $(3,-8)$. X-intercepts require solving $(x-3)^2-8=0$.

Alignment / evidence

Exact target: I can critique whether a polynomial expression supports a claimed vertex, intercept, axis of symmetry, or direction of opening.

Representation: Equation + Prose

Expected evidence/job: Independent alternate-form secure evidence for U6-MG04: Connect polynomial and quadratic expression structure to graph features such as vertex, intercepts, symmetry, and direction of opening.

Source basis: Unit 6 Assessment Plan Summative mastery evidence + Algebra 1 Framework Unit 4 supporting learning

Mastery Goal: U6-MG04 — Connect polynomial and quadratic expression structure to graph features such as vertex, intercepts, symmetry, and direction of opening.

sum_quad_structure_features

U6_SUM_V1_Q08

U6_SUM_V1_Q08 · Secure Summative V1 · Mastery · sum_quad_vertex_from_standard · v1-7 · Equivalent equations + Graph features · constructed/open response

Rewrite $f(x)=x^2-6x+5$ in vertex form. Then use that form to state the vertex, axis of symmetry, and the minimum value of $f$.

Answer / solution

$f(x)=(x-3)^2-4$. The vertex is $(3,-4)$, the axis of symmetry is $x=3$, and the minimum value is $-4$.

Alignment / evidence

Exact target: I can construct connections between polynomial structure, quadratic graphs, and key features.

Representation: Equivalent equations + Graph features

Expected evidence/job: Independent secure evidence for U6-MG04: Connect polynomial and quadratic expression structure to graph features such as vertex, intercepts, symmetry, and direction of opening.

U6_SUM_V2_Q07

U6_SUM_V2_Q07 · Secure Summative V2 · Mastery · sum_quad_structure_features · v2-3 · Equation + Graph features · constructed/open response

For $f(x)=(x+3)(x-6)$, state the x-intercepts, direction of opening, and axis of symmetry. Explain which features came directly from structure and which required one extra step.

Answer / solution

Intercepts $(-3,0),(6,0)$; opens upward; axis $x=1.5$. The intercepts and opening come directly from factored form; the axis is the midpoint of the zeros.

Alignment / evidence

A student substitutes $b=9$ for $2x^2-9x+3=0$. Explain the sign error and state the correct coefficient map.

Answer / solution

The coefficient includes its sign: $a=2,\ b=-9,\ c=3$.

Alignment / evidence

Exact target: I can design a coefficient map that organizes $a$, $b$, and $c$ before using the quadratic formula.

Representation: Equation + Prose

Expected evidence/job: Demonstrate the mapped intro learning job.

Source basis: Unit 6 Assessment Plan + Algebra 1 Framework v6.34

etb_r_pattern_reason

U6_S4_ETB_04

U6_S4_ETB_04 · Exit Ticket B · Review · etb_r_pattern_reason · etb-4 · Expression + Prose · constructed/open response

Explain why $x^2+24x+144$ fits a perfect-square-trinomial pattern.

Answer / solution

The first and last terms are squares and the middle term is $2(x)(12)=24x$, so it is $(x+12)^2$.

Alignment / evidence

Exact target: I can design a strategy for factoring special polynomial patterns.

Representation: Expression + Prose

Expected evidence/job: Short independent Review evidence for the routed prior-unit skill.

Source basis: Unit 6 Assessment Plan Review evidence route + Algebra 1 Framework source section 5.3

U6_SUM_V6_Q08

U6_SUM_V6_Q08 · Secure Summative V6 · Mastery · sum_alt_transform_sqrt_constraints · v6-8 · Equation + Key point · constructed/open response

Design an equation from the parent $y=\sqrt{x}$ with endpoint $(2,-1)$, reflected across the $x$-axis, and vertically stretched by a factor of $3$. State the transformations and give the image of the parent point $(1,1)$.

Answer / solution

A matching equation is $y=-3\sqrt{x-2}-1$. The transformations are right $2$, reflect across the $x$-axis, vertical stretch by $3$, and down $1$. The parent point $(1,1)$ maps to $(3,-4)$.

Alignment / evidence

Exact target: I can design transformed graphs using stretches, compressions, reflections, and translations of parent functions.

Representation: Equation + Key point

Expected evidence/job: Independent alternate-form secure evidence for U6-MG02: Represent translations, stretches, compressions, and reflections of parent functions across graphs, equations, tables, and key points.

Source basis: Unit 6 Assessment Plan Summative mastery evidence + Algebra 1 Framework Unit 4 supporting learning

Mastery Goal: U6-MG02 — Represent translations, stretches, compressions, and reflections of parent functions across graphs, equations, tables, and key points.

sum_transform_equation_point

U6_SUM_V1_Q06

U6_SUM_V1_Q06 · Secure Summative V1 · Mastery · sum_transform_point_table_infer · v1-5 · Table + Equation + Key points · constructed/open response

The table pairs points on the parent $y=x^2$ with their images on a transformed graph. Determine the coordinate transformation rule, write the transformed equation, and explain how one row verifies your rule.

Parent pointImage point
$(-2,4)$$(0,3)$
$(0,0)$$(2,1)$
$(2,4)$$(4,3)$
Answer / solution

The rule is $(x,y)\to(x+2,\frac12y+1)$. This is a vertical compression by $\frac12$, right $2$, and up $1$. A matching equation is $y=\frac12(x-2)^2+1$. Any listed row correctly verifies the rule.

Alignment / evidence

Exact target: I can apply transformation rules to key points and features across function families.

Representation: Table + Equation + Key points

Expected evidence/job: Independent secure evidence for U6-MG02: Represent translations, stretches, compressions, and reflections of parent functions across graphs, equations, tables, and key points.

Source basis: Unit 6 Assessment Plan Summative mastery evidence + Algebra 1 Framework Unit 4 supporting learning

Mastery Goal: U6-MG02 — Represent translations, stretches, compressions, and reflections of parent functions across graphs, equations, tables, and key points.

etb_r_gcf_reasonableness

U6_S3_ETB_03

U6_S3_ETB_03 · Exit Ticket B · Review · etb_r_gcf_reasonableness · etb-3 · Expression + Prose · constructed/open response

A student writes $22x^2-33x=11(2x^2-3x)$. Is this fully factored by the GCF? Explain.

Answer / solution

No. The GCF of both terms is $11x$, so a fuller factorization is $11x(2x-3)$.

Alignment / evidence

Exact target: I can determine whether a rewritten expression is reasonable by checking common factors and equivalent products.

Representation: Expression + Prose

Expected evidence/job: Short independent Review evidence for the routed prior-unit skill.

Source basis: Unit 6 Assessment Plan Review evidence route + Algebra 1 Framework source section 5.1

I can determine whether solutions make sense in relation to a graph or context.

context_solution_reasonableness

U6_S1_EX_04

U6_S1_EX_04 · Notes Example · Intro · context_solution_reasonableness · example-4 · Context + Equation · constructed/open response

A rectangle has side lengths $x+2$ and $x-5$. Its area is 0. Solve $(x+2)(x-5)=0$ and state which value can represent a side-length boundary.

Answer / solution

The algebraic solutions are $x=-2,5$. If side lengths must be nonnegative, $x=5$ is the meaningful boundary for $x-5$; $x=-2$ makes the other factor zero but is not a positive length setting.

Alignment / evidence

Exact target: I can determine whether solutions make sense in relation to a graph or context.

Representation: Context + Equation

Expected evidence/job: Teacher-facing worked instructional anchor for the mapped I-can.

Source basis: Unit 6 Assessment Plan + Algebra 1 Framework v6.34

U6_S1_YTI_04

U6_S1_YTI_04 · Notes YTI · Intro · context_solution_reasonableness · yti-4 · Context + Equation · constructed/open response

A product model is $(x-1)(x+7)=0$. Solve and identify which solution is nonnegative.

Answer / solution

The solutions are $x=1,-7$; the nonnegative solution is $x=1$.

Alignment / evidence

A student reports $x=4$ and $x=9$ as solutions of $f(x)=0$. Use the graph to assess the report and give the correct solutions.

Answer / solution

$x=4$ is supported by the graph, but $x=9$ is not. The graph crosses the $x$-axis at $x=4$ and $x=11$, so the correct solutions are $x=4,11$.

etc_m_family_evidence_strength

U6_S4_ETC_04

U6_S4_ETC_04 · Exit Ticket C · Mastery · etc_m_family_evidence_strength_table · etc-4 · Table + Prose · constructed/open response

A rough sketch is described only as “curved.” The table below gives values from the same relationship. Identify the familiar function family and explain why the table is stronger evidence than the word “curved.”

$x$$y$
$-2$$5$
$-1$$2$
$0$$1$
$1$$2$
$2$$5$
Answer / solution

Quadratic. The table has constant second differences for equally spaced inputs, which is diagnostic evidence; many nonquadratic graphs can also be curved.

Alignment / evidence

Exact target: I can determine which representation gives the strongest evidence for a function family when features seem similar.

Representation: Table + Prose

Expected evidence/job: Portfolio evidence slice for U6-MG01: Identify common function families using evidence from graphs, equations, tables, contexts, and key features.

Source basis: Unit 6 Assessment Plan Mastery/portfolio evidence route + Algebra 1 Framework source section 4.1

Mastery Goal: U6-MG01 — Identify common function families using evidence from graphs, equations, tables, contexts, and key features.

sum_alt_compare_families

U6_SUM_V5_Q08

U6_SUM_V5_Q08 · Secure Summative V5 · Mastery · sum_alt_family_equation_table · v5-8 · Equation + Table + Prose · constructed/open response

For $f(x)=|x-3|+2$, complete the table for the given inputs. Then identify the function family and name the evidence from the equation or table that is most diagnostic.

$x$$f(x)$
$2$?
$3$?
$4$?
Answer / solution

The outputs are $3,2,3$. The function is absolute value. The absolute-value bars in the equation are definitive family evidence, and the table is symmetric about $x=3$.

Alignment / evidence

Exact target: I can determine which representation gives the strongest evidence for a function family when features seem similar.

Representation: Equation + Table + Prose

U6_SUM_V6_Q05

U6_SUM_V6_Q05 · Secure Summative V6 · Mastery · sum_alt_family_ratio_evidence · v6-5 · Table + Prose · constructed/open response

A graph is described only as increasing and curved. The table below comes from the same relationship. Which evidence is stronger for identifying the function family? Identify the family and explain.

$x$$y$
$0$$8$
$1$$12$
$2$$18$
$3$$27$
Answer / solution

The table is stronger evidence. The outputs have a constant ratio of $\frac32$, so the relationship is exponential. “Increasing and curved” is not unique to exponential functions.

Alignment / evidence

Exact target: I can determine which representation gives the strongest evidence for a function family when features seem similar.

Representation: Table + Prose

sum_family_evidence_compare

U6_SUM_V5_Q03

U6_SUM_V5_Q03 · Secure Summative V5 · Mastery · sum_family_table_model_select · v5-3 · Table + Equation + Prose · constructed/open response

A classmate says the data might be absolute value because the outputs decrease and then increase. Decide whether a quadratic or absolute-value model is better, write an equation that matches the table, and cite the strongest table evidence.

$x$$y$
$-2$$5$
$-1$$2$
$0$$1$
$1$$2$
$2$$5$
Answer / solution

Quadratic is the better model. The constant second differences are $2$, and $y=x^2+1$ matches all five rows. A decrease-then-increase pattern alone is not enough to distinguish quadratic from absolute value.

Alignment / evidence

Exact target: I can determine which representation gives the strongest evidence for a function family when features seem similar.

Representation: Table + Equation + Prose

Expected evidence/job: Independent alternate-form secure evidence for U6-MG01: Identify common function families using evidence from graphs, equations, tables, contexts, and key features.

Absolute value; the graph is V-shaped with a sharp vertex.

$x$$y$
$0$$5$
$1$$10$
$2$$20$
$3$$40$

etb_r_factor_zero_connection

U6_S1_ETB_03

Explain the connection among a factor $(x+7)$, the zero $x=-7$, and the graph.

Answer / solution

The factor equals zero at $x=-7$, so the function value is zero and the graph has an $x$-intercept at $(-7,0)$.

sum_alt_poly_multiply_evaluate

U6_SUM_V5_Q07

U6_SUM_V5_Q07 · Secure Summative V5 · Mastery · sum_alt_poly_area_model · v5-7 · Area model + Polynomial · constructed/open response

Complete the area model for $(2x-3)(x+5)$, then combine the four partial products to write the product polynomial.

$x$$5$
$2x$??
$-3$??
Answer / solution

The partial products are $2x^2$, $10x$, $-3x$, and $-15$. Combining them gives $2x^2+7x-15$.

Alignment / evidence

Exact target: I can formulate polynomial sums, differences, and products from area, perimeter, and expression contexts.

Representation: Area model + Polynomial

Expected evidence/job: Independent alternate-form secure evidence for U6-MG03: Add, subtract, and multiply polynomial expressions using structure, like terms, equivalent forms, and area-based representations.

Source basis: Unit 6 Assessment Plan Summative mastery evidence + Algebra 1 Framework Unit 4 supporting learning

U6_SUM_V6_Q06

U6_SUM_V6_Q06 · Secure Summative V6 · Mastery · sum_alt_poly_expand_combine · v6-6 · Polynomial expression · constructed/open response

Simplify $(x+3)(x-2)+(x^2-4x+5)-(x^2+2x-3)$. Your expanded product is needed before you can combine the expressions.

Answer / solution

$(x+3)(x-2)=x^2+x-6$. Then $x^2+x-6+x^2-4x+5-(x^2+2x-3)=x^2-5x+2$.

Alignment / evidence

Exact target: I can use structure and like terms to add, subtract, and multiply polynomial expressions.

Representation: Polynomial expression

Expected evidence/job: Independent alternate-form secure evidence for U6-MG03: Add, subtract, and multiply polynomial expressions using structure, like terms, equivalent forms, and area-based representations.

Source basis: Unit 6 Assessment Plan Summative mastery evidence + Algebra 1 Framework Unit 4 supporting learning

sum_poly_area_product

U6_SUM_V2_Q06

U6_SUM_V2_Q06 · Secure Summative V2 · Mastery · sum_poly_composite_area · v2-6 · Context + Polynomial · constructed/open response

A composite display has a main rectangle with side lengths $x+4$ and $x+1$, an attached panel with area $x^2+3x+5$, and a cutout with area $x^2+x+1$. Find the total remaining area as a polynomial. Expand the main rectangle before combining the three areas.

Answer / solution

Main area: $x^2+5x+4$. After expanding the product and computing main + panel - cutout, the remaining area is $x^2+7x+8$ square units.

Alignment / evidence

Exact target: I can use structure and like terms to add, subtract, and multiply polynomial expressions.

Representation: Context + Polynomial

Expected evidence/job: Independent secure evidence for U6-MG03: Add, subtract, and multiply polynomial expressions using structure, like terms, equivalent forms, and area-based representations.

U6_SUM_V3_Q02

U6_SUM_V3_Q02 · Secure Summative V3 · Mastery · sum_poly_area_product · v3-2 · Context + Polynomial · constructed/open response

A rectangle has side lengths $x+4$ and $x-4$. Write the area as a polynomial and explain which operation produces the result.

Answer / solution

$(x+4)(x-4)=x^2+0x-16$. Multiplication/distribution combines every term from one factor with every term from the other.

Alignment / evidence

area_optimization_context

U6_S5_EX_04

U6_S5_EX_04 · Notes Example · Intro · area_optimization_context · example-4 · Context + Equation · constructed/open response

A farmer uses 40 m of fencing for three sides of a rectangle against a barn. If the two equal widths are $x$, write and optimize the area model.

Answer / solution

Length is $40-2x$. Area $A=x(40-2x)=-2x^2+40x=-2(x-10)^2+200$. Maximum area is $200$ m² at $x=10$ m.

Alignment / evidence

Exact target: I can extend quadratic models to predict maximums, minimums, intercepts, and meaningful solution values.

Representation: Context + Equation

Expected evidence/job: Teacher-facing worked instructional anchor for the mapped I-can.

Source basis: Unit 6 Assessment Plan + Algebra 1 Framework v6.34

U6_S5_YTI_04

U6_S5_YTI_04 · Notes YTI · Intro · area_optimization_context · yti-4 · Context + Equation · constructed/open response

A rectangle has perimeter 36 m. Let one side be $x$. Write an area model and find the maximum area.

Answer / solution

Other side $18-x$. $A=x(18-x)=-(x-9)^2+81$. Maximum area $81$ m² at $x=9$.

Alignment / evidence

Exact target: I can extend quadratic models to predict maximums, minimums, intercepts, and meaningful solution values.

Representation: Context + Equation

Expected evidence/job: Immediate application of the just-modeled idea with changed values or representation.

Source basis: Unit 6 Assessment Plan + Algebra 1 Framework v6.34

wtc_projectile_features

U6_S5_WTC_01

U6_S5_WTC_01 · WTC · Intro · wtc_projectile_features · context-graph · Graph + Context · constructed/open response

A ball is launched from a platform. Its height over time is shown below.

  1. Estimate when the ball reaches its greatest height and estimate that height.
  2. Estimate when it reaches the ground.
  3. Which features of a quadratic model would make those quantities easiest to determine?
Answer / solution

The maximum occurs near $t=2$ seconds at about $21$ meters. The positive ground-contact time is a little over $4$ seconds (about $4.05$). Vertex form highlights the maximum; factored/zero information highlights ground contact.

Alignment / evidence

Exact target: I can extend quadratic models to predict maximums, minimums, intercepts, and meaningful solution values.

Representation: Graph + Context

method_selection_table

U6_S4_EX_05

U6_S4_EX_05 · Notes Example · Intro · method_selection_table · example-5 · Table + Equations · constructed/open response

For each equation, explain one structural reason the listed method is a sensible first choice.

EquationMethod
$(x-3)(x+7)=0$Factoring
$x^2+6x=2$Complete square
$4x^2+x-5=0$Quadratic formula
Answer / solution

Factored form already exposes factors; an even linear coefficient makes completing the square convenient; the final equation has no obvious simple factor pair, so the quadratic formula is dependable.

U6_S4_YTI_05

For each equation, explain one structural reason the listed method is sensible.

EquationMethod
$x^2-16=0$Factoring
$x^2-4x=12$Complete square
$5x^2+2x-3=0$Quadratic formula
Answer / solution

Difference of squares factors immediately; completing the square produces $(x-2)^2=16$ quickly; the final equation is safely handled by the quadratic formula.

wtc_method_selection

U6_S4_WTC_01

U6_S4_WTC_01 · WTC · Intro · wtc_method_selection · method-table · Table + Equations · constructed/open response

Three equations are paired with a proposed first-choice method.

EquationProposed method
$(x-4)(x+1)=0$factoring
$x^2+8x=5$completing the square
$3x^2-2x-7=0$quadratic formula
  1. For each row, identify the structural feature that makes the proposed method reasonable.
  2. Choose one equation and describe a different method that would also work.
Answer / solution

The first equation is already factored and set equal to zero. The second has leading coefficient 1 and an even linear coefficient, so completing the square is efficient. The third is not obviously factorable, so the quadratic formula is dependable. Other correct methods are possible, but efficiency depends on the visible structure.

Alignment / evidence

Exact target: I can formulate a solution-method plan based on the structure of a quadratic equation.

Representation: Table + Equations

Expected evidence/job: Preview method selection as a structural decision rather than a memorized default.

Source basis: Unit 6 Assessment Plan + Algebra 1 Framework v6.34

sum_poly_area_product

U6_SUM_V1_Q03

U6_SUM_V1_Q03 · Secure Summative V1 · Mastery · sum_poly_area_product · v1-2 · Context + Polynomial · constructed/open response

A rectangle has side lengths $x+4$ and $x-2$. Write the area as a polynomial and explain which operation produces the result.

Answer / solution

$(x+4)(x-2)=x^2+2x-8$. Multiplication/distribution combines every term from one factor with every term from the other.

Alignment / evidence

Exact target: I can formulate polynomial sums, differences, and products from area, perimeter, and expression contexts.

U6_SUM_V3_Q05

U6_SUM_V3_Q05 · Secure Summative V3 · Mastery · sum_poly_composite_area · v3-6 · Context + Polynomial · constructed/open response

A composite display has a main rectangle with side lengths $x+5$ and $x+2$, an attached panel with area $2x^2+2x+3$, and a cutout with area $x^2+4x+1$. Find the total remaining area as a polynomial. Expand the main rectangle before combining the three areas.

Answer / solution

Main area: $x^2+7x+10$. After expanding the product and computing main + panel - cutout, the remaining area is $2x^2+5x+12$ square units.

Alignment / evidence

Exact target: I can use structure and like terms to add, subtract, and multiply polynomial expressions.

sum_poly_equivalent_forms

U6_SUM_V6_Q01

U6_SUM_V6_Q01 · Secure Summative V6 · Mastery · sum_poly_equivalent_forms · v6-1 · Context + Polynomial + Prose · constructed/open response

Two equivalent area expressions are proposed for a rectangle: $(x+4)(x+7)$ and $x^2+11x+28$. Verify equivalence and explain one reason each form might be useful.

Answer / solution

Expanding gives $x^2+11x+28$, so they are equivalent. Factored form displays side factors; expanded form displays combined polynomial terms and supports direct value substitution/addition with other polynomials.

Alignment / evidence

Exact target: I can extend polynomial operations to equivalent expressions written in different forms.

Representation: Context + Polynomial + Prose

Expected evidence/job: Independent alternate-form secure evidence for U6-MG03: Add, subtract, and multiply polynomial expressions using structure, like terms, equivalent forms, and area-based representations.

Source basis: Unit 6 Assessment Plan Summative mastery evidence + Algebra 1 Framework Unit 4 supporting learning

sum_quad_structure_features

U6_SUM_V1_Q04

U6_SUM_V1_Q04 · Secure Summative V1 · Mastery · sum_quad_structure_features · v1-3 · Equation + Graph features · constructed/open response

For $f(x)=(x+2)(x-4)$, state the x-intercepts, direction of opening, and axis of symmetry. Explain which features came directly from structure and which required one extra step.

Answer / solution

Intercepts $(-2,0),(4,0)$; opens upward; axis $x=1$. The intercepts and opening come directly from factored form; the axis is the midpoint of the zeros.

Alignment / evidence

Exact target: I can predict graph features from equivalent quadratic expressions.

U6_SUM_V3_Q03

U6_SUM_V3_Q03 · Secure Summative V3 · Mastery · sum_quad_vertex_from_standard · v3-7 · Equivalent equations + Graph features · constructed/open response

Rewrite $f(x)=x^2-8x+7$ in vertex form. Then use that form to state the vertex, axis of symmetry, and the minimum value of $f$.

Answer / solution

$f(x)=(x-4)^2-9$. The vertex is $(4,-9)$, the axis of symmetry is $x=4$, and the minimum value is $-9$.

Alignment / evidence

Exact target: I can construct connections between polynomial structure, quadratic graphs, and key features.

Representation: Equivalent equations + Graph features

I can predict how changing values in an equation moves a graph left, right, up, or down.

etc_m_horizontal_sign

U6_S2_ETC_04

U6_S2_ETC_04 · Exit Ticket C · Mastery · etc_m_horizontal_sign · etc-4 · Equation + Prose · constructed/open response

Explain why $y=(x-13)^2$ shifts the graph right rather than left.

Answer / solution

The inside expression is zero at $x=13$, so the vertex moves from $x=0$ to $x=13$, which is 13 units right.

Alignment / evidence

Exact target: I can predict how changing values in an equation moves a graph left, right, up, or down.

Representation: Equation + Prose

U6_S2_P3_24

U6_S2_P3_24 · Practice Set 3 · Mastery · m_horizontal_sign · p3-24 · Equation + Prose · constructed/open response

Explain why $y=(x-6)^2$ shifts the graph right rather than left.

Answer / solution

The inside expression is zero at $x=6$, so the vertex moves from $x=0$ to $x=6$, which is 6 units right.

Alignment / evidence

Exact target: I can predict how changing values in an equation moves a graph left, right, up, or down.

Representation: Equation + Prose

Expected evidence/job: Demonstrate the mapped mastery learning job.

Source basis: Unit 6 Assessment Plan spiral Mastery route + Algebra 1 Framework source section 4.2

complete_square_solve

U6_S2_EX_02

U6_S2_EX_02 · Notes Example · Intro · complete_square_solve · example-2 · Equation · constructed/open response

Solve $x^2+6x=7$ by completing the square.

Answer / solution

Add 9 to both sides: $(x+3)^2=16$. Then $x+3=\pm4$, so $x=1,-7$.

Alignment / evidence

Exact target: I can use completing the square to solve quadratic equations and write equivalent vertex-form expressions.

Representation: Equation

eta_p_error_balance_square

U6_S2_ETA_04

U6_S2_ETA_04 · Exit Ticket A · Intro · eta_p_error_balance_square · eta-4 · Equation + Prose · constructed/open response

A student changes $x^2+12x=5$ to $(x+6)^2=5$. Identify what was omitted and correct the equation before solving.

Answer / solution

Adding 36 to complete the square must be done to both sides: $(x+6)^2=41$, so $x=-6\pm\sqrt{41}$.

Alignment / evidence

p_error_balance_square

U6_S2_P1_16

U6_S2_P1_16 · Practice Set 1 · Intro · p_error_balance_square · p1-16 · Equation + Prose · constructed/open response

A student changes $x^2+10x=4$ to $(x+5)^2=4$. Identify what was omitted and correct the equation before solving.

Answer / solution

Adding 25 to complete the square must be done to both sides: $(x+5)^2=29$, so $x=-5\pm\sqrt{29}$.

Alignment / evidence

Exact target: I can use completing the square to solve quadratic equations and write equivalent vertex-form expressions.

Representation: Equation + Prose

Expected evidence/job: Demonstrate the mapped intro learning job.

Source basis: Unit 6 Assessment Plan + Algebra 1 Framework v6.34

sum_poly_area_product

U6_SUM_V1_Q07

U6_SUM_V1_Q07 · Secure Summative V1 · Mastery · sum_poly_composite_area · v1-6 · Context + Polynomial · constructed/open response

A composite display has a main rectangle with side lengths $x+3$ and $x+2$, an attached panel with area $2x^2-x+4$, and a cutout with area $x^2+x+2$. Find the total remaining area as a polynomial. Expand the main rectangle before combining the three areas.

Answer / solution

Main area: $x^2+5x+6$. After expanding the product and computing main + panel - cutout, the remaining area is $2x^2+3x+8$ square units.

Alignment / evidence

Exact target: I can use structure and like terms to add, subtract, and multiply polynomial expressions.

Representation: Context + Polynomial

Expected evidence/job: Independent secure evidence for U6-MG03: Add, subtract, and multiply polynomial expressions using structure, like terms, equivalent forms, and area-based representations.

Source basis: Unit 6 Assessment Plan Summative mastery evidence + Algebra 1 Framework Unit 4 supporting learning

Mastery Goal: U6-MG03 — Add, subtract, and multiply polynomial expressions using structure, like terms, equivalent forms, and area-based representations.

U6_SUM_V2_Q01

U6_SUM_V2_Q01 · Secure Summative V2 · Mastery · sum_poly_area_product · v2-2 · Context + Polynomial · constructed/open response

A rectangle has side lengths $x+4$ and $x-3$. Write the area as a polynomial and explain which operation produces the result.

Answer / solution

$(x+4)(x-3)=x^2+x-12$. Multiplication/distribution combines every term from one factor with every term from the other.

Alignment / evidence

Exact target: I can use structure and like terms to add, subtract, and multiply polynomial expressions.

Representation: Context + Polynomial

Expected evidence/job: Independent secure evidence for U6-MG03: Add, subtract, and multiply polynomial expressions using structure, like terms, equivalent forms, and area-based representations.

Source basis: Unit 6 Assessment Plan Summative mastery evidence + Algebra 1 Framework Unit 4 supporting learning

Mastery Goal: U6-MG03 — Add, subtract, and multiply polynomial expressions using structure, like terms, equivalent forms, and area-based representations.

U6_SUM_V4_Q02

U6_SUM_V4_Q02 · Secure Summative V4 · Mastery · sum_poly_composite_area · v4-6 · Context + Polynomial · constructed/open response

A composite display has a main rectangle with side lengths $x+2$ and $x+6$, an attached panel with area $x^2+4x+2$, and a cutout with area $x^2+2x+5$. Find the total remaining area as a polynomial. Expand the main rectangle before combining the three areas.

Answer / solution

Main area: $x^2+8x+12$. After expanding the product and computing main + panel - cutout, the remaining area is $x^2+10x+9$ square units.
